--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score decreases by 1 for items from one source half (GOOD half), increases by 1 for items from the other half (BAD half)
- We need to identify which half is good (decreases score) and consistently bring items from that half
- The two halves are distinguished by wall color: BLUE vs GREEN
- Robots should: go to source → pick up from the correct (good) half → return to nest → deliver

**Key perceptions available:**
- Floor color: BLACK (source), WHITE (nest), GRAY (middle)
- Wall/object colors: BLUE (one source half), GREEN (other source half), RED (nest wall)
- LED colors robots emit (for signaling)

**Strategy:**
Since we don't know which half (BLUE or GREEN) decreases the score, we need to try both. The best approach: go toward one colored wall (say BLUE), then return to nest (go toward RED wall). 

**State design (3-4 states):**
1. **Explore/Go to source**: Move toward BLACK floor (source area), using GoToColor for BLUE walls
2. **Return to nest**: Once on BLACK floor, go toward RED wall (nest)
3. Optionally a state for when carrying item

Since robots automatically get items by entering a half, the key is:
- Go to BLUE half (approach BLUE colored wall)
- Return to nest (approach RED wall)
- Transition on floor color

**Round 1 had no data** - I'm generating an initial diverse population.

Let me design 14 machines:

- **Machines 1-2**: GoToColor BLUE → on BLACK floor → GoToColor RED (return) → on WHITE floor → back
- **Machines 3-4**: Same but GREEN instead of BLUE
- **Machines 5-6**: Exploration based with floor color transitions
- **Machines 7-8**: Mixed strategies
- **Machines 9-14**: Various topologies

Colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

FSM 1: 2 states - GoToColor BLUE (state0), GoToColor RED (state1)
- State 0: Go toward BLUE (clr=3), transition to state1 on BLACK floor
- State 1: Go toward RED (clr=1), transition to state0 on WHITE floor

FSM 2: 3 states - Explore, GoToColor BLUE, GoToColor RED
FSM 3: Same as FSM1 but GREEN (clr=2) instead of BLUE
FSM 4: 3 states with GREEN
FSM 5-6: Exploration-based
FSM 7-8: 4-state machines

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to guide my decisions, but since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and the structure of the problem.

## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:**
- BLUE half items decrease score (good) OR GREEN half items decrease score (good) - we don't know which
- Robots must: navigate to correct source half → return to nest → deliver
- Key: GoToColor with BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) for source, RED (clr=1) for nest return
- Floor colors: BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=middle

**Best FSM structure:**
- State 0: GoToColor target_source (BLUE or GREEN) - approach correct half
- State 1: GoToColor RED - return to nest
- Transition 0→1: on BLACK floor (arrived at source, got item)
- Transition 1→0: on WHITE floor (delivered item, start again)

**Key insight:** We need to try both BLUE and GREEN source walls. The one that gives negative scores is correct. I'll generate variants of both, plus some 3-4 state machines with extra logic (avoiding the wrong half, handling gray floor).

**For 3-state machines:** Add a state for navigating through gray floor toward source, avoiding the wrong color wall.

**For 4-state machines:** Include a "wrong source" avoidance state - if robot perceives the wrong color wall, go away from it.

I'll generate 7 BLUE-targeting and 7 GREEN-targeting machines with varying complexity.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback from the rounds, which means I need to continue exploring both BLUE and GREEN targeting strategies while diversifying my approach.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from structure:**
- The core loop is: GoToColor(source_color) → on BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED/nest) → on WHITE floor → repeat
- BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2) are the two candidate good source colors
- I should also try GoAwayColor from the BAD source color as an alternative navigation strategy
- The simplest 2-state machines (FSM1, FSM2) are likely competitive - they're clean and direct

**New ideas to explore:**
1. Instead of GoToColor(source), use GoAwayColor(RED) to move away from nest toward source - this is direction-agnostic and might work well
2. Use floor-color cascades: on WHITE→go to source, on BLACK→go to nest, on GRAY→explore
3. Try attracting to robots carrying items (using LED color as signal) - robots in nest area glow yellow (LED=4)
4. Vary transition probabilities more aggressively
5. Try using GoAwayColor(wrong_source_color) as a way to avoid the bad half

**Strategy:**
- 2 machines: exact copies of best candidate designs (elitism - both BLUE and GREEN simple 2-state)
- 4 machines: small variations on the 2-state core (tweak probabilities)
- 4 machines: 3-state with GoAwayColor from wrong source
- 4 machines: novel - GoAwayColor(RED) to move toward source, GoToColor(RED) to return

I still have no score feedback, which is unusual. I'll reason more carefully about what could work and diversify more aggressively.

## Reasoning

**Critical insight I may have missed:**
- The problem says "an item from one half decreases score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1" - we need to figure out which is which
- Both BLUE and GREEN strategies have been tried equally - without feedback, I should try strategies that work regardless of which color is good
- **Key idea**: What if we try BOTH source halves but only deliver from one? Or use avoidance of one color?

**Alternative strategies:**
1. **Symmetric foraging**: Go to source (any black floor), but only approach ONE colored wall - if that wall's items are bad, robots will score positively but we need to avoid that
2. **GoAwayColor(RED) for outbound**: Moving away from the red nest wall navigates toward the source area without color bias
3. **Use LED signaling**: Robots returning with good items emit a color, others follow
4. **Pure exploration + floor transitions**: Just use floor color transitions with exploration behavior

**New topology ideas:**
- State 0: GoAwayColor(RED) [move toward source] → on BLACK → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(BLUE or GREEN) [pick up specific item] → short delay → State 2  
- State 2: GoToColor(RED) [return] → on WHITE → State 0

Also trying: what if the robot should avoid BLACK entirely (if foraging is bad)? Score is minimized, so if delivering items INCREASES score, we want robots that DON'T forage. But the problem says one half decreases score - so we DO want to forage from the right half.

Let me try more diverse topologies and also include some "don't forage" machines as a sanity check.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been getting no score feedback for 5 rounds, which suggests the simulator is running but results aren't being shown to me. I need to reason purely from first principles and try more diverse strategies.

## Reasoning

**Fundamental rethink:**

The mission minimizes score. One source color gives -1 per delivery, the other +1. Without knowing which, I've been guessing. Let me think about what strategies are robust:

1. **If BLUE is good**: GoToColor(BLUE) → deliver → score goes negative ✓
2. **If GREEN is good**: GoToColor(GREEN) → deliver → score goes negative ✓

Both have been tried. Key question: are my FSMs actually working mechanically?

**Potential issues with my designs:**
- When on BLACK floor (source), robot gets an item automatically. Then it needs to return to WHITE (nest). My transitions trigger on BLACK with high probability - but the robot might immediately leave before fully "entering" the source half
- The transition probability should maybe be lower (allowing more time in source) or the transition condition should be different

**New ideas:**
1. Try **Stop** behavior briefly at source before returning - ensures item pickup
2. Try **Attraction** behavior near nest to cluster robots (more deliveries)
3. Try very **high probability transitions** (1.0 essentially) on floor color
4. Try **GoAwayColor(source_color)** from nest side to push toward source
5. Consider that maybe the arena layout means GoToColor doesn't work well - use floor-based navigation exclusively

**Architecture to try:**
- 3-state: Explore(gray) → GoToColor(source) when seeing source color → GoToColor(RED) on black floor → back to explore on white
- 2-state with Stop at source

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been running 6 rounds with no score feedback shown to me. I need to think more carefully about what might be going wrong or what strategies I haven't tried.

## Reasoning

**Critical reanalysis:**

I've been consistently trying GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) → on BLACK → GoToColor(RED) → on WHITE. But maybe these FSMs aren't working because:

1. **GoToColor targets robot LEDs, not wall colors!** The color parameter in GoToColor refers to the colors that OTHER ROBOTS emit via their LEDs, not the wall/floor colors. Wall colors are perceived differently.

Wait - re-reading the spec: GoToColor "moves toward the nearest object of a target colour" with clr in {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The condition C=7 "perceives a given colour with its camera" uses colours {1,2,3,4,5,6}. These likely refer to the colored blocks/walls in the arena.

**Color mapping:** The spec says colors {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Common ARGoS color mapping: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So:
- RED wall (nest side) = color 1
- BLUE walls = color 3  
- GREEN walls = color 2

This matches what I've been using. So GoToColor(3)=toward blue walls, GoToColor(2)=toward green walls, GoToColor(1)=toward red/nest wall.

**Maybe the issue is that robots also need to avoid the BAD source.** If GREEN is bad (+1 per item), robots going to BLUE might accidentally enter GREEN half too.

**New strategy: GoAwayColor from BAD source + GoToColor toward GOOD source simultaneously** - but we can only have one behavior per state.

**Try completely different approach:** 
- Use Exploration to reach source area (floor turns BLACK)
- Then GoToColor specific wall
- Return via GoToColor(RED)

Also try: what if score is already good and I just need to preserve it? Try 1-state machines as sanity check.

**Most importantly**: Try machines where robots go to source area via floor detection (BLACK) without color-guided navigation, relying on random walk to hit one half more than other.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been running 7 rounds with no score feedback. Let me fundamentally rethink my approach.

## Reasoning

**The core problem I may have missed:**

Re-reading carefully: "Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, **replacing any item it was already carrying**." This means if a robot enters the WRONG half after picking up a good item, it loses the good item and gets a bad one. This is critical!

**Also:** "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1" - we don't know which is which a priori, but the FSM that targets the correct color will score negatively.

**What if nothing is working because the robots keep entering the wrong half?** A robot doing GoToColor(BLUE) might still wander into GREEN territory before reaching the nest.

**Key insight I've been ignoring:** Once on BLACK floor with a good item, the robot should go to RED (nest) as fast as possible WITHOUT re-entering any source area. GoToColor(RED) should work for this.

**Another issue:** Maybe my FSMs have transition ordering problems. In ARGoS AutoMoDe, when multiple transitions fire, the first one wins. I should check ordering.

**Fresh strategies to try:**
1. **Avoid bad source explicitly**: After getting item (on BLACK), GoAwayColor(bad_source_color) AND GoToColor(RED) 
2. **Use neighbor signaling**: Robots near nest (white floor) signal with specific LED color, others follow
3. **Simpler is better**: The absolute simplest 2-state machines might outperform complex ones
4. **Try all color combinations** including yellow(4), magenta(5), cyan(6) as target colors in case the wall colors map differently

Let me also reconsider color IDs. Maybe: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. BLUE walls=3, GREEN walls=2, RED wall=1. This is what I've been using.

**New attempt**: Try GoToColor(4)=yellow, GoToColor(5)=magenta, GoToColor(6)=cyan as alternatives - maybe the source walls are these colors instead.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I have been running 8 rounds with no score feedback shown to me. This is a fundamental problem - I'm essentially designing blind. Let me do a complete rethink.

## Reasoning

**What I know for certain:**
- Score is minimized; one source color gives -1, the other +1
- The arena has RED wall (nest), BLUE walls (one source half), GREEN walls (other source half)
- Floor: WHITE=nest, BLACK=source, GRAY=middle

**Why might my FSMs all be scoring similarly (and possibly badly)?**

1. Maybe GoToColor and GoAwayColor in ARGoS refer to **robot LED colors**, not wall/floor colors. If so, walls aren't directly targetable with GoToColor.

2. If that's the case, then the only way to navigate to specific locations is via **floor color conditions** (C=0,1,2) and **camera color perception** (C=7).

3. **C=7 (perceives color with camera)** can detect colored objects including walls. This is used as a TRANSITION condition. But GoToColor (B=8) also uses a color parameter - if this targets LED colors, then clr=1 (red) would target robots with red LEDs, not the red wall.

**Actually**: In ARGoS e-puck with range-and-bearing, GoToColor likely uses the omnidirectional camera which can see colored blobs - both robot LEDs AND colored arena elements. So GoToColor(clr=1) would move toward ANY red object (wall or robot LED).

**New strategy**: What if I use C=7 (perceives color) as transitions more aggressively, and use Exploration (B=0) as the main movement behavior, letting floor color and color perception drive transitions?

**Also trying**: Pure floor-navigation - use only floor color (C=0,1,2) for all transitions, with exploration behavior everywhere except return leg.

**Most radical idea**: What if score is 0 because robots aren't moving effectively? Try attraction/repulsion behaviors.

Let me try completely different topologies that haven't appeared yet, plus keep the best candidates from before.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
This is the final round. I've had 9 rounds with no score feedback shown to me, which means I'm designing completely blind. Let me make my most principled attempt.

## Reasoning

**Final round - maximize diversity and coverage:**

Since I never received scores, I don't know which color (BLUE=clr3 or GREEN=clr2) is the "good" source. I also don't know if my FSM structures are mechanically correct.

**Critical final insights:**

1. **The simplest possible correct machine**: GoToColor(source) on BLACK → GoToColor(RED) on WHITE → repeat. This is my core hypothesis and I'll include both color variants.

2. **What if GoToColor doesn't reach specific walls well?** The source area is divided - BLUE walls are behind one half, GREEN behind the other. GoToColor(BLUE) should reliably pull robots to the BLUE half. This should work.

3. **What if the issue is that robots start in the center and get confused?** Add an initial exploration state.

4. **Floor-only navigation**: Use exploration to wander, floor transitions to trigger returns. This doesn't discriminate between BLUE and GREEN halves - robots will pick up random items and deliver ~50% good, ~50% bad = score ≈ 0. To get negative scores we MUST target one specific color.

5. **For the final round**: 
   - 4 machines: best simple 2-state (BLUE and GREEN, with slight probability variants)
   - 4 machines: best 3-state variants 
   - 4 machines: novel approaches including GoAwayColor from bad source combined with GoToColor good source

**One more idea**: What if I try having robots go to source via GoAwayColor(RED) - moving away from the nest wall naturally takes them toward the source? Then on BLACK floor, GoToColor(RED) to return.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
